ARM — Evidence appendix
All harvested proof points · company first, then peers

Evidence

Claims by section
business-model
  • They pay twice: once for access to the design, and again on every chip that ships.
  • Every licence structure — Arm Total Access (annual portfolio fee including the newest cores, no tape-out fee), Arm Flexible Access (low annual fee on the older portfolio plus a per-tape-out fee), a Technology Licensing Agreement (fixed fee, one design) and an Architecture Licence Agreement (fixed fee, right to build a custom Arm-ISA CPU) — obliges the licensee to pay a per-chip royalty afterwards (20-F FY2026, Item 5.A).
  • Excluding the Consulting Agreement, License and Other revenue was $1,694m in FY2025 and $1,603m in FY2026, a 5.4% decline against a reported +25.4%.
  • None of those items is triggered by a licensee shipping another chip, which is why cost of sales was $121m in both FY2025 and FY2026 while royalty revenue rose $445m.
  • R&D crossed the royalty line in FY2024 and has stayed above it. The royalty annuity no longer funds the R&D that creates its successor; licence revenue does, and licence revenue covered 83% of FY2026 R&D.
  • This effect is permanently negative and Arm has never sized it. This is the mechanical explanation for the flat decade in royalty per chip
  • Across the whole range, royalty per chip rose 31% to 53% in three years while units grew roughly 1% to 6% a year.
  • Per $1,000 of chip ASP: CPU IP earns roughly $50 of gross profit at a ~5% royalty rate; a Neoverse CSS earns roughly $100 at ~10%; selling the whole chip earns roughly $500 at ~50% gross margin
  • Management guides the first generation to "high 30% range, maybe low 40s" (Q4 FY26 call; reaffirmed Q1 FY27), which implies cost of goods of $3,190-$3,410 a socket.
  • Arm has close to 100% incremental gross margin and has run negative operating leverage for two years.
  • Non-GAAP operating margin declined from 46.7% (FY2025) to 43.0% (FY2026) to 41.2% in the latest quarter. Non-GAAP operating expense grew 32.9% in FY2026 against revenue growth of 22.8%.
competitive-advantage
  • Arm's position in the CPU architecture is a real structural advantage and an unusually durable one, but it is a weaker economic asset than its market position implies, and it is being defended by escalating reinvestment rather than converted into cash returns.
  • A licensee that abandons the Arm instruction set does not lose Arm's design work; it loses compatibility with the software its own customers already run.
  • Roughly 50% of current royalty revenue comes from products launched more than ten years ago, and Arm is still collecting on designs from the early 1990s
  • In the quarter ended 2026-06-30 Arm's royalty grew 22.2% to $715m while Qualcomm's QTL licensing revenue — levied on the same handset base in the same quarter — declined 3.0% to $1,278m
  • GAAP ROIC was 16.3% in FY2026 against 28.3% in FY2023, and the three-year incremental return on invested capital (FY2023 to FY2026) was 6.4% — $181m of incremental after-tax operating profit on $2,849m of incremental invested capital, below any reasonable cost of capital for an all-equity, high-beta business.
  • Royalty growth over that period came from Arm-based chip value growing, not from Arm taking a larger cut of it.
  • The price is set low enough to be uninteresting to attack: roughly $0.89 per smartphone applications processor, under 0.3% of a $300–$400 handset, and roughly 1.8% of the value of Arm-based chip content. The low take is the defence, and it is also why 99% share does not translate into Visa-like returns.
  • No individual buyer can exercise that power without stranding its own software base, which is why the concentration shows up in rate negotiation rather than in defection.
  • Arm's gross-margin premium over the peer median is roughly 46 points and stable. Its operating margin is below Cadence, Qualcomm, NVIDIA, Broadcom and TSMC, and its R&D intensity is the highest in the table by 21 points.
  • A licensing business that converts a 97.5% gross margin into an 18.3% operating margin - 7.1% in the latest quarter - is spending its entire structural advantage on defence and expansion.
  • Nothing in the data shows a rival taking price or share from the royalty line. The margin and return compression is internal - R&D and share-based compensation - not competitive.
  • Dolby's revenue went from $1,281m (FY2021) to $1,349m (FY2025) because it has no equivalent of the Armv9-to-CSS ladder to sell, and its operating margin declined from 45-50% in 2008-2009 to the high teens today, not because a competitor displaced it but because the device base stopped growing while it kept spending.
competitive-landscape
  • Those three figures are Arm's, on Arm's definitions, and the third has no defined denominator — cores, sockets, vCPU-hours and spend give materially different answers, and Arm has not said which it means.
  • Of the roughly $6.5bn increase in Arm-share cloud chip value, roughly $5.0bn came from the market doubling and roughly $1.5bn from the three-point share gain
  • Data-centre CPU is $50bn of $330bn of cloud-AI silicon in FYE26 (15.2%) and more than $100bn of more than $1.2tn in FYE31 (8.3%). Absolute CPU royalty can compound at 20% while Arm's share of the AI compute dollar falls by roughly half.
  • $15bn of $100bn is roughly 15% of the entire data-centre CPU market by FYE31, from zero in FY2026. For comparison, AMD launched EPYC in 2017 and needed until Q1 2026 — nine years — to reach 46.2% of x86 server CPU revenue and 27.4% of total server CPU units, with a mature x86 software base, an established OEM channel and a direct price-performance comparison.
  • Arm's Q1 FY2027 licence line of $574m is 1.26x Synopsys Design IP's whole quarter and 2.4x Cadence's entire semiconductor-IP business. Nobody is closing the revenue gap.
  • The licensing model captures roughly a tenth of the value of the chips it enables in its fastest-growing end market, and no amount of share gain inside the licensing pool can close that.
  • On the metric Arm chose to compete on — cores per socket — the first-generation AGI CPU is mid-pack, not a leader: AMD Venice and Ampere MX at 256, Qualcomm Dragonfly at more than 250 (2028), Graviton5 at 192, EPYC Turin top bin at 192 and Intel Diamond Rapids at 192 all sit above it, and Cobalt 200 (132), Vera (88), Axion (72) and Grace (72) sit below.
  • Arm keeps the socket and the ecosystem; it loses the implementation uplift that is the entire source of its royalty-per-chip growth.
  • The near-term threat is not RISC-V revenue; it is RISC-V as the reason a Flexible Access prospect never signs. Arm Flexible Access net customer additions - the on-ramp for start-ups and new business units, exactly the RISC-V recruiting ground - declined from +92 in FY2025 to +15 in FY2026, down 84%, in a year when design activity was rising.
critical-points
  • External-customer revenue grew 7.4% while related-party revenue grew 82.1%, and related parties supplied 74% of the year's revenue increase.
  • The EPS target corroborates it independently: revenue from $4,920m to $25,000m is a 38.4% compound rate and non-GAAP EPS from $1.77 to more than $9 is 38.5%. Earnings are planned to grow at the same rate as revenue, not faster. Revenue rises 5.1x; margin rises one point.
expectations
  • Consensus FY2031 revenue of $25,435m sits within 2.2% of management's $25,000m target and consensus FY2031 EPS of $9.93 within 10% of "more than $9" — and only 15 to 16 of the 47 covering analysts publish an FY2031 estimate at all.
  • The pattern is consistent across fourteen years: forecasts of share in markets Arm already dominates have been accurate, and forecasts of share in new markets have been directionally right and five to seven years late. The FYE31 model rests almost entirely on the second kind — cloud, robotics, and a first-generation silicon product.
forward-model
  • FY2026–FY2031 revenue CAGR of 30.9%: royalty 17.1%, licence 9.5%, chip from zero. Non-GAAP EPS reaches management's "more than $9" FY2031 target in FY2035, four years late.
  • FY2026 actual 43.0%, base case FY2031 41.1%. Management's own disclosed FY2031 floors arithmetically produce 44.0%. Consensus has 52.2%. Three of those four agree that revenue rising 4–5x buys roughly zero consolidated margin, because the incremental revenue arrives at less than half the incumbent business's margin.
management-claims
  • What is not in dispute is that a third of Q1 FY2027 licence revenue and 15.0% of total revenue came from the controlling shareholder's affiliate.
  • If 20% of $15bn of FY2031 chip revenue displaced units a licensee would otherwise have shipped, forgone royalty at a roughly 10% CSS rate is $300m, 3.0% of the $10bn IP target; at 50% displacement it is $750m
  • Consensus non-GAAP operating income and gross margin jointly imply operating expense compounding at 9.9% from FY2027 to FY2031 while revenue compounds at 43.2%, decelerating to +5.1% and +3.3% in the final two years.
  • Weighted by domain, the uncontracted portion is concentrated where the volume is: Cloud AI is roughly 80–90% contracted through FYE31 and Physical AI roughly 95%, while Edge AI — 78% of FYE26 royalty — falls to roughly 45% contracted by FYE31
risks
  • Arm sells finished CPUs into the same hyperscale accounts whose licence fees and royalties are the business, and the dispute mechanism is already running through regulators, originating with a customer that is 9% of revenue.
  • The mechanism — an architecture licensee replacing the licensor in-house — is exactly the one Arm's largest customers are best placed to execute, since Apple, Qualcomm, NVIDIA, Amazon, Google and Microsoft all already design their own cores under Arm architecture licences.
  • Revenue is a percentage of what Arm China earns from its own PRC sublicences and is recognised net, so Arm's PRC revenue is not PRC market revenue and a change in the undisclosed revenue-share percentage would be indistinguishable from a change in PRC demand.
secular-trends
  • Arm has no exposure to the memory pricing producing that boom: its royalty is a percentage of the CPU chip's price or a fixed fee, and it licenses no memory IP. Comparing Arm to "semiconductor industry growth" is a category error in both directions.
  • Arm grows like the bottom of the AI cohort, not the top — below Cadence, an EDA vendor that sells no AI silicon — and once the related-party consulting line is removed it is six points below Cadence.
  • Non-mobile royalty was approximately $1,171m in FY2024 and $1,171m in FY2025 — 0% growth, bounded between −1.7% and +1.9% across the full rounding range, so all of FY2025's +20% royalty growth came from mobile. Then in FY2026 non-mobile grew roughly 27% while mobile grew 13%.
  • Management's statement that "the largest driver of royalty growth was cloud AI" is defensible for the June-2026 quarter — where doubling a roughly $82m base would supply about 63% of the quarter's $130m royalty increase — and is not true for FY2026 as a whole, where the Edge bucket contributed more than half on any reasonable mapping.
  • Agentic AI raising CPU intensity per data-centre watt is the claim the whole silicon case rests on: roughly 30 million CPU cores per gigawatt today rising to roughly 120 million once agentic workloads dominate, because agents issue more than 15x the requests of humans and run continuously, so CPU orchestration becomes the bottleneck ahead of accelerator throughput
technology
  • What none of them replaced was the software base attached to the ISA. Where Arm competes on the standard its position has survived thirty-five years of attack; where it competes on the design it competes with Apple's, Amazon's, NVIDIA's and Qualcomm's CPU teams.
  • A CSS is a pre-integrated, pre-verified configuration of CPU, GPU, NPU, interconnect and system IP, delivered as RTL or as GDSII already physically implemented for a named foundry process (20-F FY2026, Item 4.B).
  • The disclosed per-core pricing ladder runs approximately 1.0x for first-generation Armv9 (2023 launches), 1.5x for subsequent-generation Armv9 (2025), 2.0x for first-generation CSS and 3.0x for subsequent-generation CSS
  • Arm's entire moat is the cost of porting software. If that cost approaches zero, so does the moat.
  • Standing law is that a company can acquire an Arm architecture licensee, absorb its CPU design work, and continue shipping under its own pre-existing licence. That is the exact manoeuvre Arm tried to block.
  • It discloses no attach rate, no revenue and no share for any of them, in the FY2026 20-F, in any shareholder letter, or in any investor deck.
value-chain
  • Selling the finished CPU raises the capture to roughly 11% of cloud-AI silicon ($24bn of Arm's own $210–330bn cloud-AI figure) at roughly 50% gross margin. That is the entire strategic logic of the AGI CPU, stated in Arm's own numbers.
  • Arm captures about 0.20% of the handset wholesale dollar against above-99% share of the applications-processor market. Qualcomm's cellular patent royalty, on a narrower position, is roughly 5x Arm's per device.
  • Realized royalty rises only through mix migration, never through repricing. That asymmetry is the structural weakness of the licensing layer in an input-cost shock, and it is why a memory shock reaches Arm as a volume loss with no price offset.
  • Arm is not without leverage — it is the architecture supplier to most of the parties competing for the same capacity, and its volumes are tiny relative to a single month of N3 output — but it has no contractual claim on allocation that it has disclosed.
  • Every named AGI CPU customer already has an Arm CPU option. AWS has Graviton5 at 192 cores, Google has Axion, Microsoft has Cobalt, NVIDIA has Vera.

They pay twice: once for access to the design, and again on every chip that ships.

business-explainer · business-model · ↩ back to dossier

Company

filingsupports
Arm licenses can yield royalties for many years. Regardless of the license model a customer uses, we receive a per-unit royalty fee on substantially every chip shipped. Because each chip may ship for many years, and each Arm CPU can be reused in new products as new applications emerge, these licensing agreements help to ensure a long tail of recurring royalty revenues, which provide significant visibility into future revenue streams. Arm is still collecting royalty revenues for products that were first developed and licensed in the early 1990s.
— Arm Holdings plc IPO prospectus (Form 424B4), 2023-09-14
filingsupports
Today, any company can make a modern computer chip through the unique combination of our energy-efficient CPU IP and related technologies and our unmatched ecosystem of technology partners, and do it cost effectively due to our flexible business model. Each CPU product can be licensed to multiple companies, leading to economies of scale that allow us to charge each licensee only a fraction of what it would cost them to develop internally, while minimizing their risk and time-to-market.
— Arm Holdings plc IPO prospectus (Form 424B4), 2023-09-14
filingsupports ✦ best
Our primary business is licensing our IP products to semiconductor companies, OEMs, CSPs and other organizations to design their chips. Our customers license our IP products for a fee, which gives them access to our designs and enables them to create Arm-based chips. Once a chip has been designed and manufactured with our products, we receive a per-unit royalty on substantially all chips shipped. The royalty has typically been based on a percentage of the ASP of the chip or a fixed fee per unit, and it typically increases as more Arm products are included in the chip.
— Arm Holdings plc 20-F, fiscal year ended March 31, 2026, 2026-05-26
filingsupports
Most of our licenses have two components: license fees and support and maintenance fees (recognized as license and other revenue) and per-chip royalties (recognized as royalty revenue). However, some licenses can have multiple payment milestones that are date-based or event-based (e.g., six months after the effective date of the contract or upon tape-out of the first chip design).
— Arm Holdings plc 20-F, fiscal year ended March 31, 2026, 2026-05-26
filingcontradicts
We anticipate continued challenges from current and new competitors, including established technologies such as the x86 architecture, a family of instruction set architectures (“ISAs”), as well as by free, open-source technologies, including the RISC-V architecture. Many of our customers are also major supporters of the RISC-V architecture and related technologies. If RISC-V-related technology continues to be developed and market support for RISC-V increases, our customers may choose to utilize this free, open-source architecture instead of our products.
— Arm Holdings plc 20-F, fiscal year ended March 31, 2026, 2026-05-26
filingqualifies
As of March 2026, we also expanded our offerings to include Arm-designed silicon products with the introduction of the Arm AGI CPU, with production expected by the end of calendar year 2026. Our business model enables the widest range of customers with a broad set of options to access Arm products through an agreement best suited to their particular business needs.
— Arm Holdings plc 20-F, fiscal year ended March 31, 2026, 2026-05-26
presentationsupports
We put a lot of effort and engineering into it. But we've already seen massive benefits in terms of the customer base. We introduced this 3 or 4 years ago. Our business model is a license plus royalty. Royalty is the laggard. So royalties start to show up 2, 3 years after we license a product. Already, CSS represents almost 20% of our royalties and growing.
— Rene Haas, CEO, Arm Holdings plc Shareholder/Analyst Call (Arm Everywhere), 2026-03-24

Peer: Cadence Design Systems

filingqualifies
We generally license our design IP under nonexclusive license agreements that provide usage rights for specific designs. Some customers enter into non-cancelable commitments, whereby the customer commits to a fixed dollar amount over a specified period of time that can be used to purchase from a list of products or services. In addition, for certain IP license agreements, we collect royalties as our customers ship their product that includes our IP to their customers.
— Cadence Design Systems Form 10-K, fiscal year ended December 31, 2025, 2026-02-19

Peer: Qualcomm

filingqualifies
QTL licensing revenues consist primarily of per-unit royalties. Licensees pay quarterly royalties based on their sales of products incorporating or using our licensed intellectual property. Per-unit royalties are generally based upon a percentage of the wholesale (i.e., licensee's) selling price of complete licensed products, net of certain permissible deductions (including transportation, insurance, packing costs and other items), with certain products subject to per unit minimums and/or per unit caps.
— QUALCOMM Incorporated Form 10-K, fiscal year ended September 28, 2025, 2025-11-05

Peer: Synopsys

filingqualifies
We typically license Synopsys IP products under nonexclusive license agreements that provide usage rights for a specific number of designs. Fees under these licenses are typically charged on a per design basis plus, in some cases, royalties.
— Synopsys, Inc. Form 10-K, fiscal year ended October 31, 2025, 2025-12-22

Every licence structure — Arm Total Access (annual portfolio fee including the newest cores, no tape-out fee), Arm Flexible Access (low annual fee on the older portfolio plus a per-tape-out fee), a Technology Licensing Agreement (fixed fee, one design) and an Architecture Licence Agreement (fixed fee, right to build a custom Arm-ISA CPU) — obliges the licensee to pay a per-chip royalty afterwards (20-F FY2026, Item 5.A).

business-explainer · business-model · ↩ back to dossier

Company

filingsupports
A very small number of companies want to design customized Arm CPUs for their next-generation chips. For these companies, we can provide an architecture license which allows the licensee to develop their own CPU design that is compliant with the Arm ISA. In addition to the architecture license fee, the licensee agrees to pay a royalty on every chip that contains the Arm-compliant design.
— Arm Holdings plc IPO prospectus (Form 424B4), 2023-09-14
filingqualifies
We expect the number of new licensees for this technology to diminish over time as the effort required on their part to provide the customization often does not provide a reasonable return on investment.
— Arm Holdings plc IPO prospectus (Form 424B4), 2023-09-14
filingsupports
As we have deepened our relationships with our customers, we have started to migrate them to more holistic license agreements, including Arm Total Access and Arm Flexible Access agreements.
— Arm Holdings plc IPO prospectus (Form 424B4), 2023-09-14
filingsupports
We have a flexible business model for licensing products to customers, and we are continuously assessing our ability to provide greater flexibility to our customers and maximize the number of design wins for our products. Our customers license our products for a fee, which gives them access to our products and enables them to develop Arm-based processors. Once a processor has been designed and manufactured with our products, we receive a per-unit royalty on substantially all Arm-based chips shipped by our customers.
— Arm Holdings plc 6-K, quarter ended December 31, 2025, 2026-02-04
filingqualifies
We consider the number of extant Arm Total Access and Arm Flexible Access licenses as key performance indicators as they represent the increasing collaboration between us and our customers, which could be a leading indicator to more chips being designed with our products and, accordingly, more recurring royalty revenue in the future, improving our long-term market share.
— Arm Holdings plc Q4 FYE26 shareholder letter (6-K, Exhibit 99.2), 2026-05-06
filingsupports ✦ best
Arm Total Access Agreements: Under an Arm Total Access agreement, we license a portfolio of CPU designs and related technologies to a customer in return for an annual fee determined at execution of the agreement. We retain the right, from time to time, to add or remove specific products from the package. The agreement is for a fixed term and may limit the number of concurrent chip designs that may use products from the package.
— Arm Holdings plc 20-F, fiscal year ended March 31, 2026, 2026-05-26
filingsupports
Arm Flexible Access Agreements: Under an Arm Flexible Access agreement, we license a portfolio of CPU designs and related technologies to a customer in return for an annual fee determined at execution of the agreement. Unlike an Arm Total Access license, the package of products licensed pursuant to an Arm Flexible Access agreement will not contain our latest products. Although customers are free to experiment with products contained in the Arm Flexible Access package, they must pay a single use license fee for specific products if they include Arm products in a final chip design “tape out,” when the final result of a semiconductor chip design is sent for manufacturing.
— Arm Holdings plc 20-F, fiscal year ended March 31, 2026, 2026-05-26
filingsupports
Technology Licensing Agreements (TLA): Under a TLA, we license a single CPU design or other technology design to a customer in return for a fixed license fee. The license may be limited by term (i.e., the number of years during which the licensee is entitled to incorporate our products in new chip designs, but licensees typically have the right to manufacture designs perpetually) and/or by number of uses (i.e., the number of concurrent chip designs that may use our products). Architecture License Agreements (ALA): Under an ALA, the licensee is allowed to develop their own highly customized CPU designs that is compliant with the Arm instruction set architecture (“ISA”) for a fixed architecture license fee.
— Arm Holdings plc 20-F, fiscal year ended March 31, 2026, 2026-05-26
filingsupports
Royalty Fees: We generate the majority of our revenue from customers who enter into license agreements, pursuant to which we receive royalty fees based on average selling price of the customer's Arm-based chip or a fixed fee per chip. Royalty revenue is impacted primarily by the adoption of our products by the licensee as well as other factors, such as product lifecycles, customer's business performance, market trends and global supply constraints.
— Arm Holdings plc 20-F, fiscal year ended March 31, 2026, 2026-05-26
filingsupports
Arm Total Access agreements also provide our customers with access to a package of our products, including processor, graphics, and systems products. Arm Total Access customers have the option to license our most advanced processors as part of the package. Arm Total Access customers pay a periodic portfolio license fee to maintain access to our products. There are no additional fees payable by our customers under our Arm Total Access agreements upon tape-out because manufacturing design rights are included within the portfolio license fee and are reflected in the license pricing.
— Arm Holdings plc 20-F, fiscal year ended March 31, 2026, 2026-05-26
filingsupports
Arm Flexible Access agreements have two components: an annual low-cost portfolio license fee payable in installments and a license fee once they have reached “tape-out,” which occurs when the final result of our customer's semiconductor chip design incorporating our products is sent for manufacturing, at which point they decide which of our products they wish to deploy in their chip. We believe that Arm Flexible Access agreements are most suitable for smaller companies, including start-ups and business units of larger companies, that want to experiment with different configurations of our products before committing to a chip design.
— Arm Holdings plc 20-F, fiscal year ended March 31, 2026, 2026-05-26

Excluding the Consulting Agreement, License and Other revenue was $1,694m in FY2025 and $1,603m in FY2026, a 5.4% decline against a reported +25.4%.

margin-profile · business-model · ↩ back to dossier

Cost of sales (“COS”) is comprised primarily of the costs of providing technical support and training to our customers. Occasionally, some engineering costs may be classified as COS if one of our IP products is being customized as part of professional and design services that are directly attributable to revenue generation from customers. filingsupports
Royalties are either set as a percentage of the licensee's net sale price per chip or, less frequently, as a fixed amount per chip. In both cases, the royalty percentage typically declines as the total volume of ARM-compliant products shipped increases before typically flattening out at an agreed volume level. Royalty payment schedules in individual contracts vary depending on the nature of the license and the degree of market acceptance of the ARM architecture prevailing at the contract date. The average royalty rate reported in any period will also depend on the mix of end products into which ARM-based chips have been designed in the period.
— ARM Holdings plc (legacy, CIK 0001057997) Form 20-F, year ended December 31, 2015, 2016-02-18
filingsupports
It is also generally characterized by declining average selling prices over the life of a generation of microprocessors and physical IP libraries. The effect of these price decreases is compounded by the fact that royalty rates decrease as a function of volume. We cannot assure you that delays in licensing, poor demand for services, decreases in prices or in our royalty rates will not materially adversely affect our business, results of operations and financial condition.
— ARM Holdings plc (legacy, CIK 0001057997) Form 20-F, year ended December 31, 2015, 2016-02-18
filingsupports ✦ best
Royalties are generally either set as a percentage of the licensee's average selling price per chip or as a fixed amount per chip. The royalty rates per chip typically reduce over time as the total volume of chips incorporating our products shipped increases; notwithstanding such reductions in royalty rates and fees per chip, license agreements with component manufacturing customers typically include a minimum royalty percentage or fee per chip. Royalty payment schedules in individual license agreements vary depending on the nature of the license and the degree of market acceptance of our products on the date the license agreement is executed.
— Arm Holdings plc 20-F, fiscal year ended March 31, 2026, 2026-05-26
filingsupports
Demand for our products and services is largely dependent on the semiconductor and electronics industries, which are volatile, intensely competitive and generally characterized by declining average selling prices (“ASPs”) over the life of a generation of chips. The effect of these price decreases is compounded by the fact that our royalty revenue per chip generally decreases as the volume of sales increases, subject to an agreed minimum royalty per chip.
— Arm Holdings plc 20-F, fiscal year ended March 31, 2026, 2026-05-26
filingcontradicts
For chips where our products have provided more value, we will typically receive higher royalty revenue per chip. Accordingly, we believe that our investments in higher performance, higher efficiency, and more specialized designs will drive greater demand for our products and higher value for our customers, which is expected to result in higher royalty revenue.
— Arm Holdings plc 20-F, fiscal year ended March 31, 2026, 2026-05-26

Peer: Qualcomm

filingqualifies
Per-unit royalties are generally based upon a percentage of the wholesale (i.e., licensee's) selling price of complete licensed products, net of certain permissible deductions (including transportation, insurance, packing costs and other items), with certain products subject to per unit minimums and/or per unit caps. Certain products may also have a fixed royalty amount per unit.
— QUALCOMM Incorporated Form 10-K, fiscal year ended September 28, 2025, 2025-11-05

Across the whole range, royalty per chip rose 31% to 53% in three years while units grew roughly 1% to 6% a year.

filingsupports
Architecture License Agreements (ALA): Under an ALA, the licensee is allowed to develop their own highly customized CPU designs that is compliant with the Arm instruction set architecture (“ISA”) for a fixed architecture license fee. As the creation of an optimized CPU is very costly and time consuming, architecture licensees will often also license Arm CPU designs to use either as a complementary processor alongside the licensee's Arm-compliant CPU design, or in other chips where the licensee's own design is unsuitable.
— Arm Holdings plc Form 20-F for the fiscal year ended March 31, 2026, 2026-05-21
filingcontradicts
Additionally, our competitors may be able to use AI tools to quickly convert software written for the Arm ISA to competing ISAs, reducing the cost to enter markets where we are already established and resulting in increased competition.
— Arm Holdings plc Form 20-F for the fiscal year ended March 31, 2026, 2026-05-21
filingcontradicts
The jury concluded that certain technology was licensed to Qualcomm under the Qualcomm license and that Qualcomm had not breached the Nuvia ALA but failed to reach a verdict on whether Nuvia breached the Nuvia ALA. Both parties filed post-trial motions seeking judgment as a matter of law, and we also sought a new trial on the issues that were tried. On September 30, 2025, the Court affirmed the jury verdict on the issues on which it reached a conclusion and granted Qualcomm judgment as a matter of law in its favor to conclude that Nuvia did not breach the Nuvia ALA. We have filed an appeal with the United States Court of Appeals for the Third Circuit, which is currently pending.
— Arm Holdings plc Form 20-F for the fiscal year ended March 31, 2026, 2026-05-21
filingsupports
Arm has the world's largest ecosystem of third-party software and hardware partners, including chip design and verification tools vendors, advanced fabrication, operating system and application vendors, software tools providers, training and support services companies, and more than 22 million developers. The wide deployment of chips based on the Arm architecture provides software and tools companies with a large market to develop and sell their products into. As creating a new product or service is difficult without all the elements of the supporting hardware and software ecosystem, the breadth of the Arm ecosystem creates a virtuous cycle that deeply integrates us into the design cycle.
— Arm Holdings plc Form 20-F for the fiscal year ended March 31, 2026, 2026-05-21
filingsupports
We invest in creating leading products that can be used across a wide range of end markets and customers. As we expect to license our products to multiple customers, we can typically cover the entire cost of developing new Arm products by charging each customer only a portion of the total development costs. This lowers the costs for each semiconductor designer to license Arm products, versus developing the technology in-house, and enables customers to focus resources on differentiation. In addition, by licensing Arm CPU technology, the licensee immediately gains access to the vast Arm ecosystem, which would be impossible to leverage if they developed their own CPU in-house.
— Arm Holdings plc Form 20-F for the fiscal year ended March 31, 2026, 2026-05-21
filingsupports
The Arm compute platform is the most pervasive architecture in the world and supports a global community of more than 22 million developers. Our platform runs the vast majority of the world's software, including the operating systems and applications for smartphones, tablets and PCs, data centers and networking equipment, and vehicles, as well as the embedded operating systems in devices such as smart cameras, thermostats, drones and industrial robotics.
— Arm Holdings plc Form 20-F for the fiscal year ended March 31, 2026, 2026-05-21
filingsupports
For our IP products, our customers can choose from several licensing models, each with its own pricing mechanics and level of access to Arm IP products. These include CSS, Arm Total Access, Arm Flexible Access, TLAs and Architecture Licenses. Regardless of the license model a customer uses, we receive a per-unit royalty fee on substantially every chip shipped. Because each chip may ship for many years, and Arm compute platforms can be reused in new products as new applications emerge, these licensing agreements contribute to a long tail of recurring royalty revenues.
— Arm Holdings plc Form 20-F for the fiscal year ended March 31, 2026, 2026-05-21

— Arm Holdings plc Form 20-F for the fiscal year ended March 31, 2026, 2026-05-21

filingsupports
Royalties are generally either set as a percentage of the licensee's average selling price per chip or as a fixed amount per chip. The royalty rates per chip typically reduce over time as the total volume of chips incorporating our products shipped increases; notwithstanding such reductions in royalty rates and fees per chip, license agreements with component manufacturing customers typically include a minimum royalty percentage or fee per chip.
— Arm Holdings plc Form 20-F for the fiscal year ended March 31, 2026, 2026-05-21
filingcontradicts
Royalty revenue increased $445 million, or 21%, during the fiscal year ended March 31, 2026 as compared to the fiscal year ended March 31, 2025, driven by an improved mix of products with higher royalty rates per chip, such as Armv9 technology.
— Arm Holdings plc Form 20-F for the fiscal year ended March 31, 2026, 2026-05-21
filingsupports
Demand for our products and services is largely dependent on the semiconductor and electronics industries, which are volatile, intensely competitive and generally characterized by declining average selling prices (“ASPs”) over the life of a generation of chips. The effect of these price decreases is compounded by the fact that our royalty revenue per chip generally decreases as the volume of sales increases, subject to an agreed minimum royalty per chip.
— Arm Holdings plc Form 20-F for the fiscal year ended March 31, 2026, 2026-05-21

The price is set low enough to be uninteresting to attack: roughly $0.89 per smartphone applications processor, under 0.3% of a $300–$400 handset, and roughly 1.8% of the value of Arm-based chip content. The low take is the defence, and it is also why 99% share does not translate into Visa-like returns.

Peer: Qualcomm

filingsupports
QTL licensing revenues consist primarily of per-unit royalties. Licensees pay quarterly royalties based on their sales of products incorporating or using our licensed intellectual property. Per-unit royalties are generally based upon a percentage of the wholesale (i.e., licensee's) selling price of complete licensed products, net of certain permissible deductions (including transportation, insurance, packing costs and other items), with certain products subject to per unit minimums and/or per unit caps.
— QUALCOMM Incorporated Form 10-K for the fiscal year ended September 28, 2025, 2025-11-05
filingsupports
From time to time, companies initiate various strategies to attempt to negotiate, renegotiate, reduce and/or eliminate their need to pay royalties to us for the use of our intellectual property. These strategies have included: (i) litigation, often alleging infringement of patents held by such companies, patent misuse, patent exhaustion, patent invalidity or unenforceability of our patents or licenses, alleging that we do not license our patents on fair, reasonable and nondiscriminatory (FRAND) terms, or alleging some form of unfair competition or competition law violation ... (v) lobbying governmental regulators and elected officials for the purpose of seeking the reduction of royalty rates or the base on which royalties are calculated
— QUALCOMM Incorporated Form 10-K for the fiscal year ended September 28, 2025, 2025-11-05

filingqualifies
In addition to the architecture license fee, the licensee agrees to pay a royalty on every chip that contains the Arm-compliant design. As the creation of an optimized CPU is very costly and time consuming, architecture licensees often also license Arm CPU products to use either as a complementary processor alongside the licensee's Arm-compliant CPU design, or in other chips where the licensee's own design is unsuitable. We expect the number of new licensees for this technology to diminish over time as the effort required on their part to provide the customization often does not provide a reasonable return on investment.
— Arm Holdings plc 424B4 IPO prospectus, 2023-09-14
filingsupports
Our customers may decide to license our ISA and develop their own processors based on our ISA, rather than utilize our predeveloped products through an implementation license, resulting in less fees paid to us. Customers may choose to develop their own processors if they believe they can do so more effectively than us or if supply and capacity constraints within the semiconductor industry further incentivize vertical integration in an effort to secure additional control over their supply chains. ... If our customers, and particularly one or more key customers from whom we generate a significant portion of our total revenues, elect to develop their own processors based on our ISA, the market for our developed processor portfolio would decline, which could have a material adverse effect on our business, results of operations, financial condition and prospects.
— Arm Holdings plc 20-F, fiscal year ended March 31, 2026, 2026-05-26
filingsupports
Increasing cost and time-to-market pressures mean some customers may be better served by the integration of our IP into a subsystem. For chips where our products have provided more value, we will typically receive higher royalty revenue per chip. Accordingly, we believe that our investments in higher performance, higher efficiency, and more specialized designs will drive greater demand for our products and higher value for our customers, which is expected to result in higher royalty revenue.
— Arm Holdings plc 20-F, fiscal year ended March 31, 2026, 2026-05-26

filingqualifies
Under an ALA, the licensee is allowed to develop their own highly customized CPU designs that is compliant with the Arm instruction set architecture (“ISA”) for a fixed architecture license fee. As the creation of an optimized CPU is very costly and time consuming, architecture licensees will often also license Arm CPU designs to use either as a complementary processor alongside the licensee's Arm-compliant CPU design, or in other chips where the licensee's own design is unsuitable.
— Arm Holdings plc 20-F, fiscal year ended March 31, 2026, 2026-05-26
filingsupports
Further, several of our customers are creating their own bespoke accelerators for artificial intelligence (“AI”) workloads that are intended to sit alongside the Arm core. If, over time, more of the workloads move to accelerators away from the CPU, the CPU market size may decrease, resulting in a decrease of our license fees and royalties.
— Arm Holdings plc 20-F, fiscal year ended March 31, 2026, 2026-05-26

The pattern is consistent across fourteen years: forecasts of share in markets Arm already dominates have been accurate, and forecasts of share in new markets have been directionally right and five to seven years late. The FYE31 model rests almost entirely on the second kind — cloud, robotics, and a first-generation silicon product.

filingcontradicts
Architecture License Agreements (ALA): Under an ALA, the licensee is allowed to develop their own highly customized CPU designs that is compliant with the Arm instruction set architecture (“ISA”) for a fixed architecture license fee. As the creation of an optimized CPU is very costly and time consuming, architecture licensees will often also license Arm CPU designs to use either as a complementary processor alongside the licensee's Arm-compliant CPU design, or in other chips where the licensee's own design is unsuitable.
— Arm Holdings plc Form 20-F, fiscal year ended March 31, 2026, 2026-05-26
filingsupports
Our IP business relies on our customers to design, manufacture and sell chips incorporating our products in order to generate royalty revenue. ... However, our customers are not contractually obligated to design, manufacture, sell or deploy chips using our products on an exclusive basis or at all. Some of our existing customers design, manufacture, sell and deploy chips based on competing technology, including their own, and other existing or potential customers may do so in the future. To the extent that our customers elect to license technology from competitors, our competitive position could be adversely affected and we could lose market share. Furthermore, under many of our arrangements with customers, there is generally no minimum purchase obligation or guaranteed revenue stream.
— Arm Holdings plc Form 20-F, fiscal year ended March 31, 2026, 2026-05-26
filingsupports
We anticipate continued challenges from current and new competitors, including established technologies such as the x86 architecture, a family of instruction set architectures (“ISAs”), as well as by free, open-source technologies, including the RISC-V architecture. Many of our customers are also major supporters of the RISC-V architecture and related technologies. If RISC-V-related technology continues to be developed and market support for RISC-V increases, our customers may choose to utilize this free, open-source architecture instead of our products.

As such, revenue presented by the Company is the net amount calculated as a percentage of license and royalty fees earned by Arm China from sub-license arrangements entered into with end customers.
— Arm Holdings plc IPO prospectus, Form 424B4, 2023-09-14

Limited (“Arm China”) acts as the Company's exclusive IP distributor in the People's Republic of China ... under the intellectual property licensing arrangement (“IPLA”) and subsequent amendments. Originally, it had the right to sell technology from Arm headquarters to customers in China. Under Wu, Arm China also developed its own intellectual property. This led to two business segments: a distribution business licensing Arm's IP, and a design business selling Arm China's own lower-end IP. Arm's filings did not disclose details on Arm China's business breakdown. However, analysts believe distribution of Arm's IP generates the majority of revenue.
— Yelin Mo, Max A. Cherney and Stephen Nellis, Reuters, Arm's China relationship complicates IPO, Reuters, 2023-08-22
websupports
Almost a quarter of Arm's revenue comes from an entity it does not control but nonetheless relies on to access China's massive smartphone market. ... Virtually all of that revenue comes from Arm China, an independent entity that has the exclusive rights to distribute Arm's technology in the country. That makes Arm China, not better-known names like Apple or Qualcomm, Arm's largest customer.
— Yelin Mo, Max A. Cherney and Stephen Nellis, Reuters, Arm's China relationship complicates IPO, Reuters, 2023-08-22

Arm has no exposure to the memory pricing producing that boom: its royalty is a percentage of the CPU chip's price or a fixed fee, and it licenses no memory IP. Comparing Arm to "semiconductor industry growth" is a category error in both directions.

Agentic AI raising CPU intensity per data-centre watt is the claim the whole silicon case rests on: roughly 30 million CPU cores per gigawatt today rising to roughly 120 million once agentic workloads dominate, because agents issue more than 15x the requests of humans and run continuously, so CPU orchestration becomes the bottleneck ahead of accelerator throughput

long-term-target · secular-trends · ↩ back to dossier

Company

earnings callsupports
As AI is moving from human-based queries to continuous agent-driven workloads, this shift is expanding the role of the CPU. These Agentic workloads require CPUs to coordinate tasks, move data, manage memory, enforce security and orchestrate workaround accelerators. As Agentic AI scales, data centers will require more than 4x today's CPU capacity, creating a data center CPU market opportunity of more than $100 billion by 2030.
— Rene Haas, CEO, Arm Holdings plc Q4 FYE26 earnings call, 2026-05-06
earnings callqualifies
And the more cores you have, in theory, the more batches you can run. So our viewpoint is very much one of more cores is better. And that's why I think you're going to see increasingly larger core counts in these CPU chips. So you'll see more CPUs cores being shipped. You may not see 3x the chips, but the chips will be more expensive, which is why when people look at $100 billion TAM in 5 years or $125 billion, whatever the number is, largely, it's going to be driven by the fact that these CPU chips are going to have lots of CPU cores, which will drive ASP up.

What none of them replaced was the software base attached to the ISA. Where Arm competes on the standard its position has survived thirty-five years of attack; where it competes on the design it competes with Apple's, Amazon's, NVIDIA's and Qualcomm's CPU teams.

filingsupports ✦ best
Under an ALA, the licensee is allowed to develop their own highly customized CPU designs that is compliant with the Arm instruction set architecture (“ISA”) for a fixed architecture license fee. As the creation of an optimized CPU is very costly and time consuming, architecture licensees will often also license Arm CPU designs to use either as a complementary processor alongside the licensee’s Arm-compliant CPU design, or in other chips where the licensee’s own design is unsuitable.
— Arm Holdings plc Form 20-F, fiscal year ended March 31, 2026, 2026-05-26
filingsupports
Every CPU has an architecture, which defines the software instructions that can be executed by the CPU. The architecture is essentially a common language for software developers. It sets the foundation for a large library of compatible software which runs on those CPUs, including our Kleidi libraries that help developers optimize their AI applications on Arm chips.
— Arm Holdings plc Form 20-F, fiscal year ended March 31, 2026, 2026-05-26
filingsupports
We have maintained market share in the mobile applications processor market of greater than 99% for many years, by virtue of all key mobile operating systems depending on Arm processors. Our royalty revenue from the mobile applications processors market constituted approximately 43% of our total royalty revenue for the fiscal year ended March 31, 2026.
— Arm Holdings plc Form 20-F, fiscal year ended March 31, 2026, 2026-05-26
filingsupports
Arm has the world’s largest ecosystem of third-party software and hardware partners, including chip design and verification tools vendors, advanced fabrication, operating system and application vendors, software tools providers, training and support services companies, and more than 22 million developers. The wide deployment of chips based on the Arm architecture provides software and tools companies with a large market to develop and sell their products into. As creating a new product or service is difficult without all the elements of the supporting hardware and software ecosystem, the breadth of the Arm ecosystem creates a virtuous cycle that deeply integrates us into the design cycle.
— Arm Holdings plc Form 20-F, fiscal year ended March 31, 2026, 2026-05-26
filingqualifies

Selling the finished CPU raises the capture to roughly 11% of cloud-AI silicon ($24bn of Arm's own $210–330bn cloud-AI figure) at roughly 50% gross margin. That is the entire strategic logic of the AGI CPU, stated in Arm's own numbers.

Qualcomm's licensing program for cellular essential patents applies an effective running royalty rate of 3.25% of the selling price of branded multi-mode (3G/4G/5G) handsets... Qualcomm's rates are capped at $500 for the selling price of the phone. More specifically, the cap means $16.25 per handset for 5G SEPs for multimode phones on handsets that cost $500 or more... — Very quietly, Qualcomm seems to have published its proposed 5G rate structure for mobile handsets (Lexology), 2026-08-04

Realized royalty rises only through mix migration, never through repricing. That asymmetry is the structural weakness of the licensing layer in an input-cost shock, and it is why a memory shock reaches Arm as a volume loss with no price offset.

Cloudflare's Gen 12 servers are powered by a 4th generation AMD EPYC Processor, paired with 384 GB of DDR5 RAM, 16 TB of NVMe storage, a dual-port 25 GbE NIC, and two 800 watt power supply units.
— Cloudflare's 12th Generation servers — 145% more performant and 63% more efficient (Cloudflare blog), 2026-08-04

Peer: Qualcomm and Meta

websupports
Qualcomm revealed a CPU for data centers called Dragonfly C1000 in June 2026, with Meta announced as a major customer when production starts in 2028... The Dragonfly C1000 is a 250-core server CPU built on the Oryon architecture, with sustained frequencies above 5GHz, PCIe Gen 7, CXL support, and full enterprise RAS... Qualcomm entered into a multi-year partnership with Meta for the deployment of the Dragonfly CPU, with the C1000 set to power Meta's next-generation server fleet.
— Qualcomm unveils three new data center solutions including Dragonfly C1000 CPU, set to be deployed by Meta (DataCenterDynamics); Qualcomm press release, Qualcomm Investor Day, June 2026, 2026-08-04
